Applying that mechanically (`= _FakeSession`) broke the test: FastAPI's `dependency_overrides` introspects the override callable's *own* signature, and `_FakeSession.__init__`'s `questions: list[EvalQuestion] | None` parameter isn't a valid Pydantic field type (`EvalQuestion` is a raw SQLAlchemy model), so FastAPI crashed trying to build a schema for it. Reverted to the lambda with a `noqa: PLW0108` + explanatory comment — a case where a purely syntactic lint rule was wrong about *this* call site's semantics, caught by running the test suite after the "fix," not assumed safe from the lint pass alone.
  - **Net result:** `make lint` (`ruff check .` and `ruff format --check .`) and `make test` both pass cleanly; `mypy app` unchanged (same 10 pre-existing, already-catalogued findings — out of scope for a ruff-focused cleanup).
- **Reversible?:** yes throughout — the `per-file-ignores` entries are two lines in `pyproject.toml`; every code change was either a pure formatting/extraction change verified by an unchanged test result, or an additive named constant.

### 83. LangGraph checkpoint serialization warns on custom StrEnum types (deferred, not currently blocking)
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 3
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH §11
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none — an observed forward-compatibility warning, not a current failure.
- **Decision & rationale:** the real-Postgres checkpointer run above also printed `Deserializing unregistered type app.schemas.enums.ScopeLabel from checkpoint. This will be blocked in a future version` (and the same for `ObservedOutcome`) — LangGraph's msgpack-based checkpoint serializer doesn't have these project-defined `StrEnum` types in its default allow-list. Today this is a warning only; the round trip still worked correctly (verified: `scope_label` deserialized back as `'scope_1'`, matched real comparisons). Explicitly not fixed now: doing so properly means auditing every place a `GraphState` field is assigned an enum member (`scope_label`, `observed_outcome`, segment `type`, escalation `trigger_code`, ...) across every agent module built this phase and either converting to plain `str`/`.value` at each write site or registering the types with LangGraph's serializer — real work, not a one-line fix, and not something to do under checkpoint-review time pressure without deliberately re-testing each touched agent. Flagged here rather than silently left for a future session to rediscover.
- **Reversible?:** n/a — nothing implemented yet; whichever fix is chosen (plain-string state values vs. explicit serializer registration) is additive to the current design.

### 86. Phase 4 auth foundation: `POST /auth/dev-login`, real Bearer-token enforcement, and the `anonymous_client` fixture fix
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-011, ARCH-034, PRD-084, PRD-086
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** `prompt.txt`'s Phase 4 item "Auth/RBAC (clinician / admin / reviewer roles minimum)" and ARCH-011's "seeded HS256... JWT issuer for dev" describe the mechanism but not how a dev client actually *obtains* a token.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - Added `app/auth/devjwt.py` (`mint_token`/`DevJwtProvider.authenticate`, PyJWT HS256) as the real `AuthProvider` implementation behind `AUTH_PROVIDER=devjwt`, and `POST /auth/dev-login` (`app/api/routes/auth.py`) as the way to obtain a token in dev: given a seeded user's email (no password), it looks the user up via `app/auth/repository.find_user_with_roles`, mints a token embedding their real seeded roles, and writes a `login` audit event — the first real caller of `write_event`. The route is unconditionally `404` whenever `settings.auth_provider != "devjwt"`, so it structurally cannot exist in a real (OIDC) deployment rather than needing separate firewalling.
  - Added `pyjwt>=2.9` as a real dependency (network confirmed available) rather than hand-rolling HMAC signing — security-sensitive crypto code should use a vetted library. Lengthened `devjwt_signing_key`'s dev default from 19 to 38 bytes to clear PyJWT's `InsecureKeyLengthWarning` threshold for HS256 (RFC 7518 §3.2), with a loud runtime warning (`_warn_if_placeholder_key`) if the placeholder key is ever used, mirroring the existing `FileKEKProvider` DEV-KEY pattern.
  - `app/api/deps.py::current_principal` was tightened to require and verify a real `Authorization: Bearer` token via `get_auth_provider().authenticate(...)`, raising `401` on missing/invalid/expired — removing the Phase-1/2/3 permissive dev fallback (`Principal(user_id="dev-user", roles={"clinician"})`) that previously applied to *any* unauthenticated request. This is a real behavior tightening, not additive: it surfaced one regression — `tests/test_ingest_routes.py`'s `anonymous_client` fixture relied on that removed fallback to reach the *role* check (testing "clinician role, but route needs admin/service -> 403"); with no fallback, requests through it got `401` (no auth) before ever reaching `require_role`. Fixed by making `anonymous_client` explicitly override `current_principal` with a real clinician-role `Principal` (matching the pattern already used by the file's `_admin_service_principal` fixture), so the fixture again tests "authenticated, wrong role -> 403" as its name and the tests' intent require, rather than silently degrading to "no auth -> 401". Caught by rerunning the full suite (`408 passed`) after the `current_principal` change, not assumed safe.
  - `get_db`'s RLS GUC (`app.current_patient_scope`) wiring remains deliberately un-done generically in `app/api/deps.py` (documented in its module docstring) — deferred to the specific call sites holding an authoritative `patient_id` (`app.records.access`, `app.memory.patient_context` callers), since e.g. `POST /query`'s `patient_id` lives in the request body and isn't resolved yet when `get_db` runs as a dependency. Still outstanding, tracked for the rest of Phase 4.
- **Reversible?:** yes — `AUTH_PROVIDER` is a config switch; `dev-login` disappears under `oidc`; the `current_principal` tightening is the intended Phase-4 direction per ARCH-034 and matches how every other test fixture in the suite already authenticates (explicit `dependency_overrides[current_principal]`), so no other test relied on the removed fallback (confirmed via `grep` across `tests/`).

### 87. `resolve_field_effect` implemented against `records.record_field_policy`; wired into `get_patient_fields`
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-034, ARCH §4.2, PRD-084
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** ARCH §4.2/§17.3 specify `record_field_policy(role, purpose, field_path) -> allow|deny|mask` as the field-level gate but not (a) what happens when no policy row matches a given `(role, purpose, field_path)`, (b) whether a policy can be granted at coarser-than-per-field grain, or (c) what `mask` actually returns to the caller.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Fail-closed default.** `app/auth/rbac.py::resolve_field_effects` returns `deny` when no policy row matches at all — a field is never readable unless some row explicitly permits it, consistent with this phase's broader shift away from permissive defaults (`current_principal`, DEVIATIONS #86).
  - **Wildcard field_path.** A `(role, purpose, "*")` row is a fallback applied to every field path without its own exact-match row, checked only after the exact match misses. This makes seeding tractable (one row can grant "everything this role/purpose needs" instead of one row per field) while still letting an operator carve out per-field exceptions with a more specific row. Not specified in ARCH; a deliberate seeding-ergonomics choice, not required by any requirement ID.
  - **Bulk resolution.** `resolve_field_effects` (plural) fetches every `(field_path, effect)` row for a `(role, purpose)` in one query and resolves the whole requested batch in Python, rather than the single-field `resolve_field_effect` being called once per field (which would be N/2N round trips for an N-field `get_patient_fields` call). `resolve_field_effect` (singular) remains as a convenience wrapper.
  - **`mask` semantics.** `get_patient_fields` substitutes a fixed placeholder string (`app.records.access._MASKED_PLACEHOLDER`, `"[masked by policy]"`) for a `mask`-effect field's real value — present in the result (so the caller can tell "restricted" apart from "not on file", unlike `deny` which omits the key entirely) but never the plaintext. ARCH doesn't specify the placeholder shape; chosen to be unambiguous and never collide with a real clinical value.
  - **`get_patient_fields` now requires `actor_role`** (previously optional, audit-log-only) since it is the input to the policy gate. Every requested field that actually exists in the decrypted record is resolved through the policy table; `_NEVER_RETURNED_FIELDS` (identity fields) and the caller's own requested-field list remain a hard floor beneath the policy table, not overridable by any policy row (DEVIATIONS #70). The audit event's `detail` now also records `denied_fields`/`masked_fields` (only when non-empty) alongside the existing full `record_fields` (requested) list.
  - **`patient_record_agent.py`'s role selection.** `actor_role` was previously `state["roles"][0]` — harmless while `actor_role` was audit-log-only, but `Principal.roles` is a `frozenset`, so `[0]` is a per-process-hash-dependent, not meaningful, choice. Now that `actor_role` is access-determining, that ambiguity became a real bug risk (a dual-role principal could nondeterministically get gated as the wrong role). Fixed via `_select_actor_role`: prefer `"clinician"` when the principal holds it (this agent's own purpose is clinical-care field access, and `/query` already requires the `clinician` route permission), else the lexicographically-first role — deterministic either way. `retrieval_agent.py`/`escalation_agent.py` keep the old `roles[0]` pattern unchanged; their `actor_role` remains audit-label-only, not access-determining, so the same fix isn't needed there (flagged here in case a future change makes it access-determining too).
  - **No uniqueness constraint added on `(role, purpose, field_path)`.** The table has no unique/composite-key constraint (Alembic migration `ba3c23a19ce9`); a duplicate-key operator seeding mistake is possible. Not fixed now (would need a new migration + real-Postgres re-verification, out of scope for this change) — `resolve_field_effects`' query returns one `effect` per `field_path` via a plain `dict(...)` over the result rows, so a duplicate silently keeps whichever row the DB happens to return last, rather than crashing. Flagged for the seeding task (`scripts/seed_db.py` record_field_policy rows, still pending in Phase 4) to seed carefully / add the constraint then.
  - Verified against a real ephemeral Postgres (migrated to `ba3c23a19ce9`): exact-match-over-wildcard precedence, fail-closed default with zero policy rows, `mask` substitution, and `get_patient_fields` end-to-end (encrypt → policy-gate → decrypt-and-project) all confirmed with real seeded `record_field_policy` rows and a real encrypted `patient_record` row, not just offline mocks.
- **Reversible?:** yes — additive to the existing `record_field_policy` table (no schema change); `get_patient_fields`'s new required `actor_role` param is the only signature change, and its one production caller (`patient_record_agent.py`) already passed it by keyword.

### 88. RLS GUC wiring for `patient_record_agent`/`missing_info_agent`; `record_field_policy` unique constraint + seeding
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-034
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** `app/api/deps.py`'s `current_principal`/`get_db` docstrings (DEVIATIONS #86) already named "the specific call sites that hold an authoritative `patient_id`" as the plan for wiring `session_scope(patient_scope=...)`, but not which call sites those actually are.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Audited every `session_scope`/`_SESSION_SCOPE` call site** in the app (`grep` across `app/`) against which of the three RLS-protected tables (`records.patient`, `records.patient_record`, `memory.patient_context`) it could touch, and whether an authoritative `patient_id` is already known before the session opens. Only two qualify: `patient_record_agent.py` and `missing_info_agent.py` — both resolve `patient_id` from `state["patient_id"]` before opening their own session, and both read `records.patient_record` through `app.records.access`. Both now open `_SESSION_SCOPE(patient_scope=str(patient_id))`.
  - **Everything else stays unscoped, deliberately:** `retrieval_agent.py`/`stage_classifier_agent.py` never touch a patient-scoped table (corpus/Qdrant only — their own docstrings already say "NO PHI" / no `get_patient_fields` in their tool allow-list). `escalation_agent.py` writes `hitl.escalation`, not a patient-scoped table. `hitl/decisions.py::apply_decision` (called from `POST /hitl/escalations/{id}/decision`) reads/writes `memory.patient_context` for a `result_id`-derived patient, but its session is opened by the *route's* `get_db` dependency before the escalation (and therefore its patient) is even looked up — same chicken-and-egg constraint as `/query`'s `get_db`. This is also *correct*, not just unavoidable: a reviewer's HITL queue spans multiple patients by design (RBAC gates it by role, not by single-patient RLS), so this path is supposed to see across patients, matching the "fail-open when unset" design already established in DEVIATIONS #63. `POST /query`'s own `get_db` session (`app/api/routes/query.py`) only touches `memory.conversation`/`memory.message` (not RLS-protected) — and even though `body.patient_id` *is* known inside the route handler, scoping that session would do nothing for the agents' patient-table reads, since each agent opens its own separate DB connection.
  - **A related gap noticed, not fixed here:** `app.memory.patient_context.write_context` (implemented and tested in Phase 3, DEVIATIONS #72) has no caller anywhere in the live agent graph — no agent ever actually writes a `patient_context` row today, so the RLS policy on that table is exercised by tests but not yet by a real request. Flagged rather than silently left for a future session to rediscover; not fixed now — wiring it in (deciding which node writes provisional `guideline_match`/`stage_classification`/`missing_info` entries and when) is a real design task of its own, out of scope for an RLS-wiring pass.
  - **`record_field_policy` had no uniqueness constraint** (noted as deferred in DEVIATIONS #87). Seeding it (below) makes that concrete: an idempotent seed script needs a real upsert, which needs a conflict target. Added `UniqueConstraint("role", "purpose", "field_path")` to the model and a new Alembic migration (`e6bd710e1911`, autogenerated then hand-cleaned, chained after `ba3c23a19ce9`) — verified against a real ephemeral Postgres: applies cleanly, `alembic check` reports zero remaining diff against the models.
  - **`scripts/seed_db.py` now seeds `record_field_policy`.** Deliberately minimal: `(clinician, clinical_care, "*") -> allow` and `(reviewer, clinical_care, "*") -> allow`. `"clinical_care"` is the only purpose value anything in this codebase actually produces today (`patient_record_agent`'s `state.get("purpose") or "clinical_care"` default); no other purpose, and no admin-PHI-access route, exists yet to seed policy for — inventing rows for hypothetical future purposes would be exactly the kind of speculative design CLAUDE.md's conventions warn against. `clinician` and `reviewer` both get access (ARCH §17.3: reviewers need "the linked conversation context needed to rate"); `admin`/`service` get none, which the fail-closed default already enforces correctly by omission.
  - Verified end-to-end against a real ephemeral Postgres (seed script run twice — idempotent, no duplicate-key error): a `clinician` gets the real decrypted value via the seeded wildcard; an `admin` (no policy row) gets fail-closed `{}`; and a session scoped (`session_scope(patient_scope=...)`) to a *different* patient raises `PatientNotFoundError` for the real patient's record — RLS, field policy, and encryption all correctly composed in one real request path, not separately-mocked pieces.
- **Reversible?:** yes — the RLS wiring is two one-line changes at call sites already designed for this; the migration is additive (a constraint on a table with clean data, no destructive change); the seed rows are two upsertable rows, easily changed by re-running the (idempotent) script.

### 89. `GET /records/{patient_id}/fields` and `GET /records/{patient_id}` implemented; `get_patient_scoped_db` dependency added
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-080, PRD-084, ARCH §4.2, ARCH-034
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** the original 501 stubs' own docstring wrote the second route as `GET /records/{patient_id}?fields=a,b&purpose=...` — a `purpose` **query param** — but every other patient-scoped route in this codebase (`/query`, agents) takes purpose-of-use via the `X-Purpose-Of-Use` **header** (`app.api.deps.purpose_of_use`), and the stub's own function signature already used that dependency, not a query param. The docstring was simply stale/aspirational, not a deliberate alternate design.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - Kept `X-Purpose-Of-Use` (header), fixed the docstring to match — consistency across every patient-scoped endpoint beats one route inventing a second way to say the same thing.
  - **New `app.api.deps.get_patient_scoped_db(patient_id: str)` dependency**, closing the specific gap `current_principal`'s docstring (DEVIATIONS #86) and `get_db`'s (DEVIATIONS #88) both flagged: routes shaped `/.../{patient_id}/...` *do* have an authoritative `patient_id` before the DB session needs to exist — FastAPI resolves a dependency's own parameters against the request the same way it resolves the route handler's, so a dependency function that simply declares a `patient_id: str` parameter gets it from the route's path segment with no extra wiring. Verified this isn't just assumed: `tests/test_records_routes.py::test_patient_scoped_db_binds_patient_id_from_path` uses the *real* (non-overridden) `get_patient_scoped_db`, monkeypatches only `app.db.session.session_scope`, and asserts the captured `patient_scope` equals the URL's `{patient_id}`.
  - **Route-level RBAC + field-level policy stay layered, not merged:** both routes are `require_role("clinician")`-gated (matching `ROUTE_PERMISSIONS["records:read"]`, `app.auth.rbac` — reviewers get PHI context through the escalation payload, not this direct-access route) *and*, for the values endpoint, `record_field_policy`-gated per field via `get_patient_fields`. Because the route only ever admits clinicians, `actor_role` for the policy gate is hardcoded `"clinician"` rather than needing `patient_record_agent`'s `_select_actor_role` disambiguation (that agent's callers aren't role-restricted the same way).
  - **`GET /records/{patient_id}/fields` (names only) is neither policy-gated nor audited** — matches the precedent already set by `missing_info_agent`'s `get_record_field_index` use, which the `app.records.access` module docstring treats as a lower sensitivity tier than a value read (presence-only, no PHI value, no decryption). Not a new decision, just applying the existing one consistently at the API layer.
- **Reversible?:** yes — `get_patient_scoped_db` is additive (a new dependency function, no change to `get_db`); the two routes replace 501 stubs with real behavior gated exactly as documented.

### 90. `/corpus/*` routes implemented; `app.ingestion.corpus_access` added; a tool-registry enforcement gap noticed
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-004, PRD-005, PRD-016, ARCH §5.1
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** the original stubs' module docstring documented four routes, but only two (`GET /documents`, `GET /chunks/{id}`) existed even as stubs — `GET /documents/{id}/versions` and `POST /versions/{id}/withdraw` were undocumented-as-missing, not deliberately deferred. Neither `ROUTE_PERMISSIONS` (`app.auth.rbac`) nor ARCH names an explicit role for corpus reads or the withdraw action.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **New `app/ingestion/corpus_access.py`** (sibling to `app.ingestion.documents`, the write-side persistence module) — `list_documents`, `list_document_versions`, `get_chunk`, `withdraw_version`. Corpus rows are non-PHI (no `data_class`, no RLS) — no `patient_scope` concern, unlike `app.records.access`.
  - **Read routes open to `clinician`/`reviewer`/`admin`** (new `ROUTE_PERMISSIONS["corpus:read"]`); **withdraw is `admin`-only** (new `ROUTE_PERMISSIONS["corpus:manage"]`), per ARCH §17.3's "Admins manage corpus, users, config." Guideline content isn't per-patient PHI, so — unlike `/records/*` — there's no field-level policy gate here, only the route-level role check.
  - **`get_chunk` resolves regardless of the owning version's `status`.** ARCH §21c's "Withdrawn guideline after citations were issued" edge case is explicit: "citations still resolve with a 'withdrawn' badge; retrieval excludes withdrawn." Filtering `get_chunk` by version status would silently break that guarantee for exactly the case it exists to cover. Verified against a real ephemeral Postgres: a chunk fetched *after* its version is withdrawn still returns its text unchanged.
  - **`withdraw_version` is idempotent** (re-withdrawing an already-withdrawn version just re-sets the same status and writes another `config_change` audit row) — no state-machine validation added, since nothing in ARCH specifies illegal transitions here and the operation has no destructive side effect to guard against.
  - **Audited as `action="config_change"`** — the first real use of that ARCH §18 action category (previously only a name in the required set, never actually emitted — see the audit-completeness gap this same investigation surfaced, below).
  - **A broader gap noticed while locating "the real `get_chunk` implementation" (not fixed here):** `app.agents.registry.call_tool`/`AGENT_TOOLS` — the per-agent tool allow-list ARCH-034 and `tests/test_agent_registry.py` describe as a security control — is **never actually invoked by the live agent graph**. Every real agent module (`retrieval_agent.py`, `patient_record_agent.py`, etc.) imports and calls its underlying function directly (e.g. `from app.retrieval.hybrid import retrieve`), not via `call_tool("agent_name", "tool_name", ...)`. `register_tool` is called nowhere in production code either. So the allow-list is enforced only against direct `call_tool()` calls in tests, not against what the real graph actually does — a mis-wired agent module could call anything importable, and the allow-list would never see it. Not fixed here: retrofitting `call_tool()` dispatch into every agent module is a real architectural change (how each node calls its tools, not just what corpus routes return), out of scope for this pass. Flagged so it isn't silently rediscovered later as "the security control that was never wired up."
- **Reversible?:** yes — `app.ingestion.corpus_access` and the two route implementations are additive; `ROUTE_PERMISSIONS` gained two dict entries.

### 91. `POST /query` gets real `query`/`answer` audit events; `model_id` threaded through synthesis state; `/query` now actually requires `clinician`
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-035, ARCH §18
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none on the audit-event shape (ARCH §18 already specifies "one `audit_event` per query/retrieval/record-access/answer/HITL-action/ingestion/config-change/login"); the judgment calls were about how to fill fields the graph doesn't currently expose, and a scope decision about a bug found while doing this.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Auditing `POST /query`** was checked against ARCH §18's required action list and found to write **neither** `action="query"` nor `action="answer"` — despite `retrieval` (`app.retrieval.hybrid`), `record_access` (`app.records.access`), `hitl_action` (`app.hitl.decisions`/`escalation`), and `login` (`app.api.routes.auth`) all already being real. Added both: `action="query"` right after the user's message is persisted (records the *attempt* — `query_hash` only, no plaintext, matching the existing `action="retrieval"` precedent of never storing raw query text even encrypted); `action="answer"` after the graph completes, covering **both** branches (a released answer AND an escalation) with `outcome` set from `observed_outcome` either way — deliberately not a separate "escalation" audit category, since ARCH's action list doesn't name one and `outcome` already carries that distinction on the same `answer` row.
  - **`model_id` didn't exist anywhere in `GraphState`** — nothing recorded which model actually produced an answer. Threading it through required touching `app.agents.guideline_synthesis_agent`'s `_default_chat_fn`/`_resolve_chat_fn` (the one place `LLMGateway.chat()` is called and its `ChatResult.model_id` is available) to close over `state` and set `state["model_id"]`. Deliberately did **not** change `_CHAT_FN`'s test-seam contract (`Callable[[str], str]`) to also return a model id — every existing test in `test_guideline_synthesis_agent.py` sets `_CHAT_FN` directly and has no reason to care which model "answered" a stubbed response, so widening that contract would have forced every test double to fake a field it doesn't use. `_default_chat_fn`/`_resolve_chat_fn` are both private (`_`-prefixed); no test touches them directly, confirmed via `grep` before changing their signatures.
  - **A real access-control gap found and fixed while adding these events:** `POST /query` depended on bare `current_principal` (any authenticated role), not `require_role("clinician")` — despite `app.auth.rbac.ROUTE_PERMISSIONS["query:submit"]` already documenting `{"clinician"}` as the contract. Any authenticated principal (reviewer, admin, even a `service` account) could submit clinical queries. Fixed to `require_role("clinician")`, matching the documented contract and every other role-gated route in this codebase. This also simplified the new audit events' `actor_role`: since the route now guarantees `clinician`, no `_select_actor_role`-style disambiguation is needed (same reasoning as `/records/*`, DEVIATIONS #89).
  - **Still not closed:** ARCH §18's `ingestion` action category — `app/api/routes/ingest.py`/`app/ingestion/tasks.py` write no audit event for a document or record-batch ingestion, the last of the four action categories this investigation found completely unimplemented (`query`, `answer`, `config_change` are now real; `ingestion` isn't). Not fixed in this pass — flagged rather than silently left.
- **Reversible?:** yes — `write_event` calls are additive; the `model_id` state field and `_default_chat_fn` closure are additive; the `require_role` tightening matches an already-documented (if previously unenforced) contract, so it's a bug fix, not a new policy.

### 92. `ingestion` audit events added to all four `/ingest/*` routes — ARCH §18's action taxonomy now fully implemented
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-035, ARCH §18
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** ARCH §18 doesn't specify the `detail` payload shape for an `ingestion` event, or whether a document upload and a record-batch upload should share one action name or get their own.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **One shared `action="ingestion"`** for both document and record ingestion, distinguished by `detail["kind"] ∈ {"document", "records"}` — mirrors how `action="answer"` already distinguishes released-vs-escalated via `outcome` rather than a separate action name (DEVIATIONS #91). `detail` otherwise carries what an operator would need to find the ingested thing again without decrypting anything: for documents, `document_id`/`document_version_id`/`content_sha256`; for records, `source`/`data_class`/`dataset_id`/`count` — never patient identifiers or record content.
  - **`_guarded_ingest`** (the function shared by all three record-ingestion routes: `/records/file`, `/records/eav`, `/records`) writes the one event, so all three get it from a single call site rather than three duplicated ones.
  - **Threading `actor_id`/`actor_role` required changing three routes from `dependencies=[Depends(require_role(...))]` to a bound `principal: Principal = Depends(require_role(...))` parameter** — the former never gives the handler a way to read who the caller was. `/records/file` and `/records/eav` (admin-or-service routes) needed a deterministic role choice for a principal that could hold both — added `_select_actor_role(roles, *preferred)` in `app/api/routes/ingest.py`, same reasoning as `patient_record_agent._select_actor_role` (DEVIATIONS #87) and not shared code with it (different module, different role-preference order, not worth a cross-cutting abstraction for two call sites).
  - **This closes the last of the four ARCH §18 action categories found unimplemented** while auditing this endpoint family (`query`, `answer`, `config_change` closed in DEVIATIONS #90/#91) — every action ARCH §18 names now has at least one real writer: `query`, `retrieval`, `record_access`, `answer`, `hitl_action`, `ingestion`, `config_change`, `login`.
  - Verified against a real ephemeral Postgres: a standalone `write_event(action="ingestion", ...)` call chains correctly (genesis `prev_hash`, real `row_hash`) — the route-level integration (audit call following a real `ingest_records`/`create_or_supersede_document_version` call) is exercised by the (now-updated) offline route tests, which already cover that path's logic; only `write_event`'s own DB-chaining behavior needed a fresh real-DB check.
- **Reversible?:** yes — additive `write_event` calls; the `dependencies=[...]` -> bound `principal` parameter change is a no-op for route behavior (FastAPI resolves the same dependency either way), only exposing the already-required principal to the handler body.

### 93. `GET /admin/audit` implemented (`app.audit.log.query_events`); audit-reading itself deliberately left unaudited
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-086, ARCH-034, ARCH-035
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** the old `admin.py` module docstring asserted "audit access (itself audited)" with no backing `ARCH-###`/`PRD-###` id, and ARCH §18's 8-category action taxonomy (query/retrieval/record_access/answer/hitl_action/ingestion/config_change/login) has no obvious fit for "an admin listed some audit rows" — it didn't change anything (`config_change` is wrong) and it isn't a PHI field read (`record_access` is wrong, and ARCH-034's "admin access to PHI... requires a purpose and is audit-logged" is specifically about admin reads that go through `/records/*`, not about reading audit metadata).
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **New `app.audit.log.query_events(session, *, action=None, patient_id=None, actor_id=None, limit=50)`** — most-recent-first, `limit` capped at `_MAX_QUERY_LIMIT=200` server-side regardless of what's requested (an unbounded query against a table that only ever grows is its own footgun). Sibling to the existing `_fetch_last_row_hash`/`_fetch_all_events`/`verify_chain`, but a new, public, filtered read path — the other two are internal to `write_event`/`verify_chain`'s own needs (respectively "the very last row" and "every row, for a full chain walk"), neither fits an admin-facing filtered listing.
  - **`GET /admin/audit`** takes `action`/`patient_id`/`actor_id`/`limit` query params plus an optional `verify=true` that also runs `verify_chain` and returns any broken row ids in the same response — a deliberate, minimal answer to DEVIATIONS #50's still-open "a scheduled chain-verifier job... is also Phase 4" note: an on-demand admin-triggered check, not a Celery-beat schedule (that's a bigger design question, see the Celery/async item still pending for this phase). Response rows never include `query_text_enc`/`response_text_enc` (raw ciphertext — not JSON-serializable and not useful without a decrypt step this listing has no reason to perform).
  - **Reading the audit log is deliberately NOT itself given a new audit event.** Rather than force-fit an existing action category or unilaterally invent a 9th one (the schema column is a plain `String(32)`, not a DB-enum, so it's *possible* — DEVIATIONS #72 already added a novel value once — but that doesn't mean it's warranted here without a concrete ARCH/PRD id backing it), the module docstring's "itself audited" claim was corrected to explain why it doesn't apply as originally worded, rather than silently implementing something not actually required. Flagged for a future session/operator to decide if this needs its own category.
  - Fixed a stale inline comment while in this area: `app/db/models/audit.py`'s `action` column comment said `ingest`; ARCH §18's actual prose says `ingestion` (matching what `app/api/routes/ingest.py`/`app/ingestion/corpus_access.py` now actually write, DEVIATIONS #90/#92) — corrected, no functional change (the column has no CHECK constraint enforcing either spelling).
  - Verified `query_events`' actual filtering/ordering/limit-capping against a real ephemeral Postgres (offline tests use a WHERE-clause-blind fake, matching this suite's established pattern, so the real filtering logic needed a real-DB check, not just trusted from the SQL read).
- **Reversible?:** yes — `query_events` and the route are additive; nothing about existing audit writers changed.

### 94. Celery async handling for long agent runs: additive `POST /query/async` + `GET /query/jobs/{job_id}`, synchronous `/query` unchanged
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-007, PRD-105
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** `prompt.txt`'s Phase 4 checklist item ("Redis/Celery async task handling for ingestion and long-running agent runs") and PRD-105 ("Redis + Celery... for async ingestion and long-running agent tasks") don't specify the HTTP contract for the "long-running agent runs" half — ingestion already had one (`process_document` since Phase 2); the query/agent-graph path didn't. Concretely undecided: should `POST /query` itself become async (breaking its existing synchronous contract), or should async be a separate, additive capability?
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Chose additive, not a breaking redesign of `POST /query`.** PRD-NFR-1 sets a **soft** ~15s p50 target "on the reference dev machine... documented, not contractually enforced" — that framing already assumes the *typical* case is a normal, synchronous HTTP request/response, not a poll loop. Forcing every query through an async job-handle contract would be over-engineering for the common case and would have broken the already-verified synchronous behavior (`test_query_route.py`, the eval harness, and presumably any future frontend all assume a direct response). Instead: `POST /query` is byte-for-byte unchanged in behavior and contract; two new routes, `POST /query/async` (202 + job handle) and `GET /query/jobs/{job_id}` (poll), give a caller that doesn't want to hold a connection open — a slow SCOPE-2 chain, a gateway fallback retry, a deliberate batch/replay tool — a real Celery-backed alternative.
  - **No `asyncio.to_thread`/blocking-in-the-event-loop complexity.** A "hybrid" design (enqueue, then block-with-timeout inside the sync route before falling back to a job handle) was considered and rejected: it would need `asyncio.to_thread` to avoid blocking the whole event loop on `AsyncResult.get()`, adding real complexity to the hot path for a benefit (occasionally saving one client a second poll) that doesn't justify it. Plain enqueue-and-return is simpler and the additive route is what actually gets exercised only when a caller opts in.
  - **Shared logic, not two copies.** Split `app.api.routes.query`'s persistence/audit/response-assembly logic out into `app.agents.query_pipeline` (`prepare_query`, `assemble_and_persist_response`) and the lazy-singleton graph invocation into `app.agents.graph_runtime` (`invoke_graph`) — both the synchronous route and the new `app.agents.tasks.run_query` Celery task call the exact same functions, so "what a completed query run persists/audits" is defined once. `prepare_query` (conversation resolution, the user's turn, the `query` audit event) runs **synchronously** even on the async path — the attempt should be recorded immediately, not only once/if the Celery run completes; only the graph invocation + response persistence move to the worker.
  - **Not idempotency-safe on task retry** — flagged explicitly in `app.agents.tasks`'s own module docstring rather than solved here: `task_acks_late`/`task_reject_on_worker_lost` (`app.worker`, unchanged) mean a worker crash mid-task re-queues it, and no idempotency key exists to stop a retry from double-writing the assistant turn/audit row. This is the same property `process_document` already has (ARCH §21c self-critique item 5 already names this class of problem); not newly introduced, not newly solved.
  - Verified end-to-end against real infrastructure, not just offline mocks: a real Redis broker (task registration confirmed in `celery_app.tasks`, a real `send_task` enqueue confirmed landing in the broker's queue) and a **real, separate Celery worker OS process** consuming from it, persisting to a real ephemeral Postgres — a dispatched task with a real `conversation_id` came back `SUCCESS` with the expected `QueryResponse` shape, and the `answer` audit event + assistant `message` row were confirmed written by the worker process via direct SQL. (The worker's own graph invocation was stubbed in this verification run only — same reasoning as other agent-layer real-Postgres passes this phase: the graph's own correctness is already covered by `test_agent_graph.py` and doesn't need re-proving through Celery specifically; what needed proving here was the dispatch/consume/persist/result-backend plumbing, which is now confirmed real.)
- **Reversible?:** yes — `POST /query/async`/`GET /query/jobs/{job_id}` and `app.agents.tasks.run_query` are wholly additive; the `query_pipeline`/`graph_runtime` extraction is a pure refactor (the synchronous route's behavior and its existing test suite are unchanged, confirmed by running the full suite before and after).

### 95. End-to-end `docker compose up` verification pass — six real bugs found and fixed, none of which any offline test or single-script real-Postgres check could have caught
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** ARCH-036, PRD-106
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none — this was a verification pass (prompt.txt's Phase 4 checklist), not a design decision. Everything below is a genuine bug, found by actually running the full stack (proxy, api, worker, postgres, redis, qdrant, llm-gateway, frontend under the `dev` profile) and driving real HTTP requests through it end to end, rather than by inspecting the compose file or re-running isolated real-Postgres scripts (the discipline used throughout this phase, which — as this pass shows — cannot catch bugs that only manifest when multiple real services and multiple concurrently-open DB sessions interact).
- **What was verified, and what broke:**
  1. **`deploy/nginx/nginx.conf`'s `/api/` proxy silently 404'd every request.** `proxy_pass http://api:8000/;` (trailing slash) strips the `/api/` prefix nginx matched on — but the FastAPI app mounts every route under `/api` (`app.main`'s `api_base_path`), so the backend never had a route at the stripped path. Every request through the proxy 404'd despite the identical path working when called directly against the `api` container. Fixed: `proxy_pass http://api:8000;` (no trailing slash) — nginx then forwards the original URI, `/api/...`, unchanged.
  2. **`POST /ingest/documents` uploads were invisible to `worker`.** Neither `api` nor `worker` mounted a volume for `SAMPLE_GUIDELINES_DIR`/`PATIENT_RECORDS_DIR` — an uploaded file landed only on `api`'s own ephemeral container filesystem, so `worker`'s `process_document` task (a *different container*) got `FileNotFoundError` trying to parse it. Fixed: added `./data/sample_guidelines` and `./data/patient_records` as bind mounts shared by both `api` and `worker` in `docker-compose.yml` — also makes uploads survive a container recreate, and matches the ingest route's own docstring ("the same directory an operator manually drops PDFs into").
  3. **The same bind mount then hit a host/container UID mismatch.** `api`/`worker` run as `user: "10001:10001"` (Dockerfile's documented non-root rationale); the host `data/sample_guidelines`/`data/patient_records` directories were `1000:1000`, mode `775` — group-writable only for gid 1000, so uid 10001 could read but not write, and the upload failed with `PermissionError`. Fixed pragmatically (dev-only, non-secret data): `chmod -R a+rwX` on both directories. A real fix that survives a fresh clone needs either a documented one-time host-prep step or an entrypoint-level chown; not fully resolved — flagged rather than left silently working only because this session happened to fix the permissions by hand.
  4. **The LangGraph Postgres checkpointer's lazy setup self-deadlocked on the very first real `/query` request.** `PostgresSaver.setup()` (triggered lazily, on first graph invocation, per `app.agents.graph_runtime`'s original design) runs `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, which — per Postgres's own documented semantics — waits for *every* other transaction open anywhere in the database to finish, not just ones touching the same table. The first real request's own `get_db()` session had an uncommitted `query` audit-event INSERT sitting open (the whole request was one long transaction) at the exact moment the lazy checkpointer setup ran on a separate connection — so the `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` waited on a transaction that could not close until the very request doing the waiting finished. A real HTTP request through nginx sat until the proxy's own `proxy_read_timeout` (120s) gave up with a 504; the request was, in fact, still blocked server-side afterward (confirmed: a direct request to the `api` container's own `/healthz`, bypassing nginx, also timed out — the single uvicorn process had nothing else free to serve). Fixed: warm the checkpointer once, eagerly, in `app.main`'s FastAPI `lifespan` startup hook (`app.memory.checkpointer.get_checkpointer()`/`close_checkpointer()`) — before any request can possibly hold an open transaction. Confirmed safe for the offline suite: `TestClient(fastapi_app)` used without `with` (every existing test in this codebase) does not trigger FastAPI/Starlette lifespan events, so this doesn't touch any of the 465 offline tests (verified by running the full suite before and after).
  5. **The audit hash-chain has a real concurrency race**, found immediately after fixing #4 let real requests actually complete: `GET /admin/audit?verify=true` reported a broken link. Root cause: `write_event`'s `_fetch_last_row_hash` (`app/audit/log.py`) does a plain `SELECT ... ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1` with no serialization; two `write_event` calls on **different sessions** (`POST /query`'s own `get_db()` session writing `query`, and `app.retrieval.hybrid.retrieve` — called from inside the graph run, on its own separate session, per `app.agents.retrieval_agent`'s design — writing `retrieval` moments later and committing independently) can both read the same "last row" before either INSERT is visible to the other, producing two rows that share one `prev_hash`. Reproduced directly (not just inferred from the proxy-request symptom): 8 threads, each its own real session, racing `write_event` against the real Postgres this stack was already running — reliably broke the chain. **First fix attempt — `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the last row — was tried and confirmed NOT to work**, by the same repro: locking an *existing* row only blocks a second transaction from locking that *same* row; a fresh INSERT never modifies it, so once the lock holder commits, everyone blocked on it wakes and proceeds with the now-stale row they'd already planned to use. **Actual fix:** `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (a fixed key, `app.audit.log._CHAIN_LOCK_KEY`) taken before the read — a transaction-scoped advisory lock that serializes the whole read-last-hash-then-insert sequence itself, for every caller, regardless of which specific row anyone reads; auto-releases at COMMIT/ROLLBACK. Re-ran the same 8-concurrent-writer repro against the fix: zero new breaks (the table's pre-existing broken rows from before the fix remain broken forever, correctly — audit is append-only, historical corruption can't be un-recorded, only prevented going forward).
  6. **The fix for #5 then reintroduced a variant of #4's deadlock.** `POST /query`'s route session (`get_db()`) still stayed open for the whole request, including the entire graph invocation; the advisory lock from #5 is held for the writing transaction's *full* lifetime — so the `query` event's lock, held by the still-open outer request transaction, now blocked `retrieval`'s own `write_event` call (a different, separate session) for the *whole rest of the request*, which could not finish (and release the lock) until `retrieval` — the thing it was blocking — returned. Same proxy-timeout symptom as #4, confirmed via `pg_stat_activity`: one session `idle in transaction` holding the uncommitted `query` INSERT, another `active` blocked on `Lock:advisory`. **Root architectural fix, not a narrower patch:** `POST /query`/`POST /query/async` no longer use `Depends(get_db)` at all — a single session that outlives a multi-session computation in the middle of a request is the actual anti-pattern, and it would have kept causing new variants of this same class of bug for any future code that both (a) writes audit rows early in a request and (b) later calls something that opens its own separate session and also writes an audit row, no matter how the specific lock/serialization mechanism worked. Now uses two short-lived, explicit `_SESSION_SCOPE()` calls (a new module-level test seam matching every agent module's existing `_SESSION_SCOPE` convention) — one for `prepare_query` (commits and closes *before* the graph runs), one for `assemble_and_persist_response` (opened only *after* the graph returns) — so no transaction this route holds ever overlaps the graph invocation, eliminating the whole class of hazard rather than the one manifestation. Verified: 5 real concurrent `/query` requests through the full stack, all fast (20-55ms) and correct; `GET /admin/audit?verify=true` afterward showed zero new breaks.
  - **Also fixed while verifying:** `.env.example`'s `DEVJWT_SIGNING_KEY` was still the pre-Phase-4 19-byte placeholder (`dev-only-change-me`), inconsistent with `app/config.py`'s own default already lengthened to 38 bytes this phase (DEVIATIONS #86) — a real deployment following `.env.example` would still have hit PyJWT's `InsecureKeyLengthWarning` on every login. Updated `.env.example` to match; confirmed the warning disappears with a real login through the running stack.
  - **What was confirmed working, unmodified:** TLS termination + HTTP→HTTPS redirect (self-signed dev cert per `deploy/tls/README.md`); `alembic upgrade head` and `scripts/seed_db.py` (including this phase's new migration `e6bd710e1911` and `record_field_policy` seed rows) run cleanly via `docker compose exec api`; `POST /auth/dev-login` end to end; full document ingestion (`POST /ingest/documents` → `process_document` on `worker` → real Qdrant collection/points) once #2/#3 were fixed; `POST /query` and `POST /query/async`/`GET /query/jobs/{id}` producing correct, well-formed responses (disclaimer always present) against a real ingested corpus; `GET /admin/audit` with real accumulated events across `login`/`query`/`retrieval`/`answer`/`ingestion` actions and working hash-chain verification.
- **Reversible?:** yes throughout — nginx config line, two new bind-mount volume entries, a `chmod`, a startup-hook reordering, a locking-primitive swap, and a session-scoping change in one route module; none is a schema or external-contract change. All six are also each independently the *kind* of finding that would keep recurring for any future request-handling code with a similar shape (a session held open across a multi-session sub-computation) — flagged accordingly in the code comments at each fix site, not just here.

### 96. Checkpoint 4 self-review caught a real regression `pytest`/`ruff` couldn't: `app.eval.harness` importing a name deleted by the Celery-async refactor
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 4
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-105, ARCH §16
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none — a real bug, found by running `make typecheck` (not part of this session's routine check loop until now) as part of preparing the Checkpoint 4 self-review.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **`app/eval/harness.py::_invoke_pipeline`** still did `from app.api.routes.query import _invoke_graph` — a name that stopped existing the moment DEVIATIONS #94's refactor moved the lazy-singleton graph invocation into `app.agents.graph_runtime.invoke_graph` and left only `_GRAPH_INVOKE_FN = invoke_graph` (a bound reference, not a function definition) in `query.py`. This is a real break in the eval harness's actual pipeline-invocation path (`make eval` / `python -m app.eval.run`): the first real (non-monkeypatched) call would raise `ImportError`. Neither `pytest` nor `ruff` caught it — every test of `_invoke_pipeline` monkeypatches `_INVOKE_PIPELINE_FN` entirely (per the function's own comment, "most callers... never invoke the real pipeline"), so the broken import line was never executed by the offline suite. `make typecheck` (`mypy app`) did catch it (`attr-defined`), but wasn't part of this session's routine per-change verification loop (`ruff check`/`ruff format`/`pytest`) — it was only run now, while preparing this checkpoint's self-review. Fixed: import `invoke_graph` from `app.agents.graph_runtime` instead — also a strictly lighter import than the old one (no FastAPI/route-module dependency at all), improving on the original deferred-import rationale, not just restoring it.
  - **Also fixed two smaller Phase-4-introduced mypy regressions** found by the same `make typecheck` run: `app.auth.rbac.resolve_field_effects`'s `rows = dict(session.execute(...))` needed an explicit `dict[str, str]` annotation (mypy couldn't infer it through the SQLAlchemy `Row` overload); `app.api.routes.admin._serialize_event`'s parameter was untyped (`# noqa: ANN001` suppressed ruff's version of the same complaint but not mypy's `no-untyped-def`) — given a proper `AuditEvent` type hint instead.
  - **`get_patient_fields`/`resolve_field_effects`/`resolve_field_effect`'s `actor_role`/`role` parameters retyped `str | None`** (were `str`, non-optional) — `patient_record_agent._select_actor_role` can legitimately return `None` (no roles in state) and this was already a handled, documented case (fails closed to `deny`, since no policy row has a `NULL` role), not an error path; the type signature was simply wrong about what the function actually, correctly does with that input. A real type-safety gap (mypy: `Argument "actor_role" has incompatible type "str | None"; expected "str"`), not a runtime bug — Python doesn't enforce parameter types, and the `None` path was already exercised correctly (SQLAlchemy's `Column == None` compiles to `IS NULL`, matching zero rows against a non-nullable column, which is exactly the intended fail-closed behavior).
  - **After these fixes, `mypy app` reports exactly the same 10 pre-existing findings across 6 files** DEVIATIONS #85 already catalogued (`app/ingestion/eav.py`, `app/logging.py`, `app/rubric/irr.py`, `app/worker.py`, `app/api/middleware.py`, `app/api/deps.py`) — confirmed by re-running `make typecheck` after the fixes above and diffing the finding count/locations against #85's list. None of those are touched by Phase 4 work; left as-is, matching the "don't fix unrelated pre-existing issues without being asked" discipline followed throughout this session.
  - **Process note:** `make typecheck` should have been part of the routine per-change verification loop this whole phase, not just run once at the end while preparing a checkpoint review — it caught a real regression nothing else in the loop could. Flagging this as a process gap for future phases, not just a one-off fix.
- **Reversible?:** yes — three targeted fixes (one import path, two type annotations), no behavior change; `make test`/`make lint` re-confirmed clean (465 passed) after.

### 97. `GET /hitl/escalations` (list) added; the ARCH §12.2 `open -> in_review` transition wired — a gap found while starting Phase 5's frontend
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 5
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-030, PRD-033, ARCH §12.2, ARCH §13
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none on the requirement itself (ARCH §12.2 explicitly documents `open → (reviewer pulls) in_review → resolved`); the gap was purely an implementation omission from Phase 3, surfaced while building the accept-axis review UI this phase.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Before this: no way for a reviewer to discover an escalation id at all.** `GET /hitl/escalations/{id}` existed (Phase 3) but requires already having an id; there was no list/collection endpoint. `GET /review-queue` (rank mode) is a *different* workflow over a *different* table (`eval.result`, not `hitl.escalation` — `Result` has no `escalation_id` column; the only link is `Escalation.trigger_detail["result_id"]`, one-directional, escalation→result, used internally by `apply_decision`). Building Phase 5's accept-axis UI made this concrete: without a list endpoint, that UI would have nothing to select from, i.e. would be effectively decorative. Fixed by adding `app.hitl.escalation.list_escalations` (mirrors `app.records.access.list_documents`'s shape) and `GET /hitl/escalations`, reviewer/admin-gated, matching `GET /review-queue`'s existing pattern exactly. Default (no `state` param): everything not `resolved` (`open` + `in_review`) — "what a reviewer would want to pull from"; an explicit `?state=` filters to exactly that value.
  - **The `open -> in_review` transition (`mark_in_review`, implemented and unit-tested since Phase 3 per its own test file, but never called from any route) is now wired**: `GET /hitl/escalations/{id}` calls it when the caller holds the `reviewer` role — "opening the detail view" *is* "the reviewer pulls it," matching ARCH §12.2's own phrasing exactly. Deliberately **not** triggered for an `admin`-only caller: an admin inspecting an item for oversight shouldn't silently pull it out of the open pool for reviewers who could actually resolve it. Not its own audited transition — matches `mark_in_review`'s pre-existing docstring, which already reasoned that creation and final resolution are the audited transitions, this is an intermediate UI-visibility state.
  - Updated the (now stale) test that asserted `GET /hitl/escalations/{id}`'s response always shows `state: "open"` — that assumption was only ever true because nothing set it to anything else; replaced with tests for both the reviewer-triggers-transition and admin-does-not-transition cases.
- **Reversible?:** yes — both additions are new functions/routes; no existing endpoint's request or response contract changed (the transition changes a *field's value* on an already-returned response, not its shape).

### 98. Phase 5 frontend: auth flow, rank-mode/accept-axis kept as two separate workflows, `partial_accept` uses full-text edits not span-level, eslint tooling fixed
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 5
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-107, ARCH-010, ARCH §13
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** prompt.txt scopes Phase 5 to "the query interface, citation display, and all three HITL interaction modes" — it doesn't specify an auth flow, whether rank mode and the accept axis should present as one combined action or two, or how `partial_accept` should let a reviewer edit an answer.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Auth:** a `/login` screen against `POST /auth/dev-login` (email only, matching the backend's own dev-only design, DEVIATIONS #86), token + `user_id`/`roles` kept in `localStorage`, attached as `Authorization: Bearer` to every call. A 401 clears the stored token and hard-navigates to `/login` (full reload, not a state-management event bus) — simplest reliable way to reset all React state for an MVP internal tool; not worth building pub-sub for. Nav links are hidden by role (`hasRole`) but this is presentation only — the backend's `require_role` is the only real enforcement point, and every backend route was independently confirmed to 401/403 correctly regardless of what the frontend shows.
  - **Rank mode and the accept axis are presented as two separate sections/workflows, not a single combined "rate + decide" action**, because the backend genuinely doesn't link them: `eval.result` (rank mode, `GET /review-queue`) and `hitl.escalation` (accept axis) are different tables with no exposed cross-reference (`Result` has no `escalation_id`; the only link, `Escalation.trigger_detail["result_id"]`, is one-directional and internal to `apply_decision`). Building a UI that implied one combined submission would misrepresent what the API can do — DEVIATIONS #79 already flagged the "both axes together" gap on the rubric-schema side (`RatingRoundRequest.accept_action` is accepted by the schema but silently dropped by `submit_rating`); this is the frontend-side consequence of that same, not-yet-closed gap. Not fixed here — a real design question (does closing it mean giving `Result` an escalation reference, or something else) out of scope for a frontend phase.
  - **`partial_accept` submits `edited_answer` (a full-text rewrite), never `span_actions`.** Checked before building: `app.agents.escalation_agent` flattens `candidate_segments` into one joined string (`"\n".join(seg["text"] for seg in segments)`) before it's ever encrypted/stored — by the time `GET /hitl/escalations/{id}` returns `candidate_answer`, the original per-segment structure (type, citation_ids, boundaries) no longer exists. A span-index editor built against this would have to invent fake segment boundaries (e.g. splitting on newlines) with no reliable correspondence to the real segments the backend's "removed spans logged as eval failures" analysis needs — presenting a feature that looks real but silently produces meaningless `span_actions`. The backend accepts `edited_answer` as an equally valid, fully-supported alternative (`HitlDecisionRequest`'s own validator), so this isn't a workaround, just the honest choice given what data actually exists.
  - **`GET /review-queue` returning `[]`** in every real check this phase (DEVIATIONS #79: no writer creates `Result` rows from a live `/query` answer yet) is expected, not a frontend bug — confirmed via a real backend call, not assumed. `ReviewQueue`/`RankModeSection` render "No results currently open for rating" rather than erroring.
  - **`eslint.config.js` didn't exist** despite `package.json`'s `lint` script referencing it (an ESLint 9 flat-config requirement; the Phase 1 scaffold predates ESLint 9's default config format change) — `npm run lint` would fail outright. Added a standard Vite+React+TS flat config (`typescript-eslint`, `eslint-plugin-react-hooks`, `eslint-plugin-react-refresh`) and fixed every real finding it then surfaced: three `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` violations (synchronous `setState` calls at the top of data-fetching effects — restructured to set state only inside the resolved `.then()`/`.catch()`, and to reset per-item form state via React `key`-driven remounting instead of a reset effect, matching current React team guidance) and one fast-refresh warning (split `useAuth`/`AuthContext` into their own file, since a file mixing a component export and a hook export defeats Vite's fast refresh).
  - **`tsconfig.json` had no `noEmit`**, so `npm run build`'s `tsc -b` step (meant only to type-check ahead of `vite build`, which does the real bundling) was actually compiling and writing a `.js` file next to every `.ts`/`.tsx` source in `src/` — invisible until this phase, because nobody had run a full `npm run build` before (the Phase 1 scaffold was never built, only read). Deleted the ~20 stray compiled files this surfaced and added `"noEmit": true` (plus `composite`/`tsBuildInfoFile` pointed at `node_modules/.tmp/`, already gitignored via the root `node_modules/` entry) so `tsc -b` goes back to being a pure type-check gate. Confirmed fixed: a clean rebuild produces zero files in `src/`.
  - **`npm audit` flags 4 moderate/high findings** (`esbuild` — dev-server-only request-forwarding issue; `react-router` — an open-redirect CVE) inherited from the Phase 1 scaffold's pinned versions. `npm audit fix --force` would bump to `vite@8`/`react-router-dom@7`, both breaking majors — not applied here without dedicated compatibility testing time; flagged rather than either silently left or silently force-upgraded.
  - **Explicitly not built, matching prompt.txt's stated Phase 5 scope** ("query interface, citation display, all three HITL interaction modes") rather than expanding it: no admin console, no corpus/document management UI, no records browser, no eval-harness UI. Phase 4 built the API for all of these; a UI for them isn't part of what Phase 5 asked for.
  - **Verified, not just built-and-assumed:** a real backend (ephemeral Postgres/Redis/Qdrant, direct `uvicorn` + `celery worker`, not the docker-compose stack — faster iteration for this pass) driven through the Vite dev server's own `/api` proxy (the same code path a browser would use) — login, `POST /query` (both a real ingested-guideline answer attempt and a real `scope_boundary` escalation), `GET /rubric/domains`, the full escalation lifecycle (`GET /hitl/escalations` list → `GET .../{id}` triggering `open → in_review` → `POST .../decision` with `out_of_scope` + `reason_code` → dropped from the default list), and both 401 (no token) and 403 (wrong role) paths — all confirmed to return exactly the shapes `src/types.ts`/`src/api/client.ts` expect. `npm run build`, `npm run lint`, and a real `docker build` of `frontend/Dockerfile` all pass clean. **Not verified: actual browser rendering/interaction** — no browser-automation tool was available in this session; CLAUDE.md's UI-testing guidance is being followed by disclosing this explicitly rather than claiming a level of verification that didn't happen.
- **Reversible?:** yes throughout — new files/routes are additive; the eslint fixes are behavior-preserving refactors (confirmed via the same manual integration pass before and after).

### 99. Correction to #98: rank mode and the accept axis are ONE combined submission per case, not two independent workflows
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 5
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-107, ARCH-010, ARCH-019, ARCH §13.2
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** #98 (and, on the schema side, #79) treated rank-mode rating and the accept-axis decision as genuinely independent — reasoning that `eval.result` and `hitl.escalation` are unlinked tables, so a UI implying one combined submission would misrepresent the API. The user then explicitly corrected this: "For each simulated case/query each ranker has to do two tasks: (1) complete the 11-domain rubric assessment for the response, and (2) select on[e] of the options on the accept axis" — i.e. for the rank-mode workflow specifically, both axes are ONE action per case, not two. Re-reading ARCH §13.2 confirmed this was always the intended design ("Both axes together") and that the data model already had the link: `eval.rating_round.accept_action_id -> hitl.hitl_decision.id` (`app/db/models/eval.py`) existed since Phase 3 but was never wired up — a real, pre-existing implementation gap (correctly flagged as a gap by #79), not a new requirement. `hitl.hitl_decision.escalation_id` is nullable, confirming a `HitlDecision` was always meant to be able to exist without any `hitl.escalation` row.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **Backend:** `RatingRoundRequest.accept_action` (`app/schemas/rubric.py`) changed from optional-and-silently-dropped to **required**, with `accept_edited_answer`/`accept_accepted_context_ids`/`accept_reason_code` alongside it and a `model_validator(mode="after")` enforcing the same cross-field rules `HitlDecisionRequest` already enforces (reason code required for `reject`/`partial_accept`/`out_of_scope`; edited answer required for `partial_accept`) — mirroring an established pattern rather than inventing a new one.
  - **`app/hitl/decisions.py`** refactored to extract the accept-axis effect logic shared by both entry points: `_apply_patient_context_effect` (provisional `PatientContext` rollback/accept/partial-accept) and `_create_hitl_decision` (the `HitlDecision` row + edited-answer encryption). `apply_decision` (the existing escalation-resolution path) rewritten to use these helpers — confirmed behavior-preserving by re-running `tests/test_hitl_decisions.py` immediately after (5/5 passed, unchanged). New `apply_rating_accept_action(session, *, result_id, message_id, reviewer_id, action, edited_answer, accepted_context_ids, reason_code)` is the rank-mode path: creates a `HitlDecision` with `escalation_id=None`, applies the patient-context effect directly against `result_id`, writes a `hitl_action` audit event with `detail={"result_id": ..., "action": ..., "reason_code": ..., "context": "rating_round"}` (the `context` key distinguishes this from an escalation-resolution audit row without changing the action name, so existing audit-event consumers/queries aren't broken).
  - **`app/rubric/workflow.py::submit_rating`** now takes the accept-axis fields as required/optional params, calls `apply_rating_accept_action` after building the `RatingRound`/`RubricRating` rows, and sets `round_row.accept_action_id = decision.id` — closing the exact link ARCH §13.2 specified. `app/api/routes/rubric.py`'s route passes the new fields through; the response now includes `accept_action_id`.
  - **The standalone escalation-resolution workflow is deliberately kept, not merged away** — `GET /hitl/escalations` → `POST .../decision` (`apply_decision`) remains a real, distinct entry point. ARCH §13.2 itself names three distinct contexts the accept axis applies to: a candidate answer held by an escalation, a released answer under `review_sampling`, or a live turn a reviewer opens — only the first of these (escalation-held) is what a single-reviewer, often-urgent resolution actually is, and it is not the multi-rater rank-mode context the user's correction was about. Conflating the two into one code path would have been the wrong fix — reusing the *effect* logic (via the new shared helpers) while keeping the two *entry points* separate is the fix that actually matches both ARCH §13.2's own framing and the user's specific correction.
  - **Frontend:** `AcceptAxisControls.tsx` converted from a self-contained form (own submit button, own state) to a pure `{value, onChange}` controlled fieldset, so the same UI can be embedded in two different parent forms without duplicating markup: `RubricForm.tsx` now embeds it directly, owns one `AcceptAxisValue` state alongside the rubric scores, and submits both together via a new `RatingSubmission` shape (`{scores, comment, accept}`) in one `POST /rubric/results/{id}/ratings` call; `ReviewPage.tsx`'s `AcceptAxisSection` now wraps it in a new `EscalationDecisionForm` component for the standalone escalation-resolution workflow, unchanged in behavior from #98 otherwise. Both parent forms reset the shared child state by mounting it with `key={itemId}` (the React-team-recommended remount pattern already established in #98, rather than a `useEffect`-driven reset).
  - **`ACCEPT_ACTIONS`/`ACCEPT_REASON_REQUIRED`/`defaultAcceptAxisValue`/`isAcceptAxisValueValid` moved out of `AcceptAxisControls.tsx` into a new plain module, `frontend/src/acceptAxis.ts`** (matching the existing `auth.ts`/`useAuth.ts` flat-file convention already in `src/`), rather than left as named exports alongside the component. Necessary, not stylistic: `AcceptAxisControls.tsx` needed to export these helpers for `RubricForm.tsx`/`ReviewPage.tsx` to share validation/defaulting logic, but a `.tsx` file mixing a component default-export with multiple named constant/function exports trips `eslint-plugin-react-refresh`'s `only-export-components` rule (added in #98) even with `allowConstantExport: true`, since that option only covers primitive-literal constants, not arrays or functions — `npm run lint --max-warnings 0` would have failed. Splitting the helpers into their own file is the same fix #98 already applied to `useAuth`/`AuthContext` for the identical reason.
  - **Verified against real Postgres** (ephemeral Docker instance, script cleaned up after): a rank-mode `submit_rating` call with `accept_action=reject` produced a real `RatingRound` row whose `accept_action_id` correctly pointed at a real `HitlDecision` row; the provisional `PatientContext` row for that result was correctly rolled back; a real `hitl_action` audit event was written with the expected `detail` shape including `"context": "rating_round"`. Backend suite: **478 passed** (was 472 before this change) — `tests/test_rubric_workflow.py`/`tests/test_rubric_routes.py` rewritten with a generic `_FakeSession` (assigns a uuid to anything with `id is None` on `flush()`, rather than type-specific branching) to cover the new required field, its validator, and the round↔decision link; `make lint` clean; `make typecheck` at the same pre-existing 10-finding baseline documented in #96 (no new findings). Frontend: `npm run build` and `npm run lint --max-warnings 0` both clean after the `acceptAxis.ts` split.
- **Reversible?:** yes — the schema/behavior change (`accept_action` required) is a breaking API change only for a caller relying on the old silently-dropped-field behavior, which nothing in this codebase did (the old value was never read); the shared-helper extraction and frontend controlled-component/module split are behavior-preserving refactors, confirmed via the existing and new test suites before and after.

### 100. A ranker's task is exactly two things — no reason code, no escalation — correcting #99's carried-over `reason_code` requirement for rank mode
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 5
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-107, ARCH-019, ARCH §13.2
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none — a direct user instruction. #99 closed the "both axes together" gap but carried over `HitlDecisionRequest`'s `reason_code`-required-for-reject/partial_accept/out_of_scope rule onto the rank-mode path unchanged, reasoning (at the time) that the two entry points should share validation rules since they share effect logic. The user then explicitly corrected this: "for each simulated case/query each ranker has to do two tasks: (1) complete the 11-domain rubric assessment ... and (2) select on[e] of the options on the accept axis. There should be no further escalation nor any other request for ranker's reasons." This makes two things explicit that #99 left implicit/wrong: (a) a ranker's task is exactly those two things and nothing else — no reason/justification text; (b) rank mode must never produce a further escalation.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **(b) was already true, structurally — verified, not just asserted.** `apply_rating_accept_action` (`app/hitl/decisions.py`) has always constructed its `HitlDecision` with `escalation_id=None` and never calls `app.hitl.escalation.create_escalation` (confirmed by grep: that function is only ever called from `app.agents.escalation_agent`, part of the `/query` graph pipeline, never from `app.rubric.workflow` or `app.rubric.tasks`). Added `tests/test_rubric_workflow.py::test_submit_rating_never_creates_an_escalation` (loops every `HitlAcceptAction`, asserts no `Escalation` row appears in the fake session) so this guarantee is a regression-tested property, not just a fact established by code reading.
  - **(a) required a real change: `accept_reason_code` is removed from the rank-mode path entirely**, not just made optional. `app.schemas.rubric.RatingRoundRequest` no longer has an `accept_reason_code` field at all (the `_ACCEPT_REASON_REQUIRED` validator branch for it is deleted; `accept_edited_answer`'s requirement for `partial_accept` is kept — that field IS the action's content, not a justification for it, so it stays required). `app.rubric.workflow.submit_rating` no longer takes an `accept_reason_code` parameter; `app.hitl.decisions.apply_rating_accept_action` is still called with `reason_code` simply omitted, defaulting to `None` (the function signature itself is unchanged — `apply_decision`, the escalation-resolution entry point, still needs the parameter). `app/api/routes/rubric.py` no longer passes the field through. Pydantic v2's default `extra="ignore"` behavior (no `model_config` override exists in this schema) means an old client still sending `accept_reason_code` in the request body doesn't error — the field is silently dropped, same as `accept_action` used to be before #99 — but nothing in this codebase sends it anymore.
  - **The standalone escalation-resolution workflow (`HitlDecisionRequest`, `apply_decision`) is deliberately left untouched** — it still requires a reason code for reject/partial_accept/out_of_scope. The user's instruction was specifically about "each ranker" completing "each simulated case/query," i.e. the rank-mode multi-rater workflow; it does not describe the different, single-item, accountability-bearing act of a reviewer resolving an urgent held escalation directly, which #99 already established (and ARCH §13.2 itself frames) as a genuinely separate context. Collapsing that distinction now, in the opposite direction from #98's over-separation, would be the same category of mistake #99 just corrected.
  - **Frontend:** `AcceptAxisControls` gained a `requireReason?: boolean` prop (default `true`, preserving existing behavior at its one other call site). When `false`, the component never renders the reason-code input and never treats it as required. `RubricForm.tsx` passes `requireReason={false}` and calls `isAcceptAxisValueValid(accept, false)`. `acceptAxis.ts`'s `isAcceptAxisValueValid(value, requireReason = true)` gained the same parameter, defaulting to preserve the escalation-resolution behavior. `ReviewPage.tsx`'s `EscalationDecisionForm` (the standalone workflow) needed no change — it relies on the preserved default. `api/client.ts`'s `submitRating` no longer sends `accept_reason_code` in the request body at all.
  - **Verified against real Postgres** (ephemeral Docker instance + a real `alembic upgrade head` to the current head migration + `scripts/seed_db.py` for the 11 rubric domains; container removed after): `submit_rating(..., accept_action=HitlAcceptAction.REJECT)` called with **no** `accept_reason_code` argument at all succeeded and produced a real `HitlDecision` row with `reason_code IS NULL` and `escalation_id IS NULL`; a direct `SELECT count(*) FROM hitl.escalation` on the same database returned `0`; the resulting `hitl_action` audit event's `detail` showed `"reason_code": None`. Offline suite: **479 passed** (was 478) — `test_rubric_workflow.py`/`test_rubric_routes.py` updated (the old `test_submit_rating_reject_requires_reason_code` replaced with `test_submit_rating_reject_succeeds_without_reason_code`, asserting `200` not `422`; new `test_submit_rating_never_creates_an_escalation`). `make lint`/`make typecheck` clean (typecheck at the same pre-existing 10-finding baseline). Frontend: `npm run build`/`npm run lint --max-warnings 0` both clean.
- **Reversible?:** yes — removing a required field only loosens the contract (no caller in this codebase relied on the old required-reason-code behavior surviving past #99, which had only just introduced it); the frontend prop addition defaults to the prior behavior at its other call site, so `EscalationDecisionForm` is unaffected.

### 101. Neither a ranker nor a reviewer resolving an escalation is required to write an edited answer for `partial_accept`
- **Date / phase:** 2026-09-14 / Phase 5
- **Requirement ID(s):** PRD-032, ARCH-019, ARCH §13.2
- **Ambiguous or undecided:** none — a direct user instruction: "The ranker / HITL should not have to provide an edited answer." Both `app.schemas.rubric.RatingRoundRequest` (rank mode) and `app.schemas.hitl.HitlDecisionRequest` (the standalone escalation-resolution workflow) had required text for `partial_accept` — `accept_edited_answer` in the former (kept required by #100's reasoning that "the edited text IS the partial_accept action, not a justification for it"), and `edited_answer` **or** `span_actions` in the latter. The user's instruction directly overturns that #100 reasoning for both entry points ("ranker" = rank mode, "HITL" = the escalation-resolution reviewer) — a `partial_accept` no longer needs any text or per-span data to be valid.
- **Decision & rationale:**
  - **What `partial_accept` still does without any text.** `_apply_patient_context_effect` (`app/hitl/decisions.py`) was already independent of `edited_answer` — for `PARTIAL_ACCEPT` it calls `partial_accept_for_result(session, result_id, accepted_ids)`, driven entirely by `accepted_context_ids`; rows in that set become `reviewer_edited`, every other still-provisional row for the result is expired. `_create_hitl_decision` already only encrypts/stores `edited_answer_enc` `if edited_answer is not None` — a `None` was already a safe, handled value, never dereferenced. So relaxing the requirement needed no change to any effect logic, only to the two Pydantic validators gating what the HTTP layer accepts — confirmed by reading both functions before touching either schema, not assumed.
  - **`app/schemas/rubric.py`:** the `_accept_action_requirements` validator's `partial_accept requires accept_edited_answer` branch is deleted (it was the validator's only remaining check after #100 removed the reason-code branch, so the whole `@model_validator` method — and the now-unused `model_validator` import — is deleted too, not just the one branch). `accept_edited_answer` stays as an optional field; a ranker may still volunteer text if they choose to.
  - **`app/schemas/hitl.py`:** the `partial_accept requires edited_answer or span_actions` branch is deleted from `_require_reason`; `reason_code`'s own requirement for reject/partial_accept/out_of_scope is untouched (that's a different field, not addressed by this instruction, and the standalone escalation-resolution workflow's reason-code requirement was deliberately preserved by #100 for the same reason).
  - **Frontend:** `isAcceptAxisValueValid` (`frontend/src/acceptAxis.ts`) drops its `partial_accept && !editedAnswer.trim()` check entirely — this was the one remaining check that applied unconditionally to both call sites (unlike the reason-code check, it never took a `requireReason`-style parameter, because until now it was believed to be substantive rather than a justification). The edited-answer `<textarea>` in `AcceptAxisControls.tsx` stays in the UI (a ranker or reviewer can still choose to write one) but its placeholder now reads "optional" and nothing blocks submission when it's empty. `api/client.ts`'s `submitRating` and `ReviewPage.tsx`'s `EscalationDecisionForm` submit handler both changed `editedAnswer` (always sent when the action was `partial_accept`, even as `""`) to `editedAnswer || undefined`, so an unwritten field is omitted from the request body rather than sent as an empty string.
